  MIPRE.QLD.swapU_conj_mTilde_Z, MIPRE.QLD.sum_snorm_sq_sub_eq_two_sub,
  MIPRE.QLD.sum_uniform_agree_bornProb_le, MIPRE.QLD.SimulPair.swapA,
  MIPRE.QLD.SimulPair.swapU_conj_mTildeAt, MIPRE.QLD.inconsistency_triangle,
  MIPRE.QLD.inconsistency_regroupVec, MIPRE.QLD.pauliAtPOVM,
  MIPRE.QLD.SimulPair.inconsistency_mTilde_pauli_le, MIPRE.QLD.abs_qform_sub_qform_le,
  MIPRE.QLD.SimulPair.bornProb_padded, MIPRE.QLD.SimulPair.inconsistency_padded,
  MIPRE.QLD.SimulPair.inconsistency_mTilde_pauli_le',
  MIPRE.QLD.inconsistency_eq_half_xPovmDist, MIPRE.QLD.inconsistency_pt_pt_le,
  MIPRE.QLD.inconsistency_pt_pauli_le,
  MIPRE.QLD.SimulPair.inconsistency_mTilde_pauli_le_of_win}
There is $\delta_{qld} = O\bigl( \delta_S^{1/4} + md/q \bigr)$ and local unitaries $V_\alice$,
$V_\bob$ on the expanded spaces such that, writing $M = 2^m$:
\begin{enumerate}
\item there is a state $\ket{\aux}$ on the two parties' non-ancilla registers with
  $\norm{ V_\alice \ot V_\bob \ket{\widehat{\psi}} - \ket{\aux} \ot \ket{\EPR_q}^{\ot M} }^2
  \leq \delta_{qld}$ --- the bound is on the \emph{squared} norm, which is why
  Theorem~\ref{thm:qld}, whose item bounds the norm itself, carries $\delta_{qld}^{1/2}$ and
  halves the constant $b$;
\item conjugation by $V_\alice$, resp.\ $V_\bob$, carries the strategy's total Pauli measurement
  $M^{(\pauli,W)}_h$ to $\Id \ot \tau^W_h$ on the EPR registers, within $\delta_{qld}$ in the
  state-dependent distance relative to $\ket{\aux} \ot \ket{\EPR_q}^{\ot M}$.
\end{enumerate}
Conjugation carries the exact Pauli observables of Lemma~\ref{lem:qld-exact-paulis} to the honest
Pauli operators \emph{exactly}, with no error at all; that identity is a step of item 2's proof,
not a weaker form of it.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The standard swap-isometry argument for item 1. It is standard \emph{because} the representation is
exact: an approximate representation would not give a unitary, which is the whole reason
Lemma~\ref{lem:qld-exact-paulis} is stated with exact relations. Item 2 is not exact and is the
longer half: it chains the win implications' Pauli-basis consistency and the point measurements'
self-consistency through the marginals of Lemma~\ref{lem:qld-helper}, and it is where the $md/q$ of
$\delta_{qld}$ comes from, by Lemma~\ref{lem:schwartz-zippel}. Item 1 alone gives
$O(\sqrt{\delta_S})$.
\end{proof}

\paragraph{Comments.} What is formalized is the whole of item 1 \emph{given its hypothesis} ---
the self-consistency of Lemma~\ref{lem:qld-pauli-selfcons}, at a uniform $\tilde{u}$ --- and none
of item 2. The exact pieces are in \texttt{MIPRE/Background/QLD/SwapUnitary.lean} and the
assembly in \texttt{MIPRE/Background/QLD/SwapState.lean}.

Every operator in the construction has the shape $\sum_p \widehat{S}_p \ot T_p$
(\texttt{sTensor}), and because $\widehat{S}$ is projective these compose factorwise
(\texttt{sTensor\_mul}). That one lemma is where projectivity is used, and it makes the rest
arithmetic: the swap map \texttt{swapU} is unitary on both sides
(\texttt{swapU\_mul\_conjTranspose}, \texttt{swapU\_conjTranspose\_mul}), and conjugation by it
strips the measurement off the observable, \texttt{swapU\_conj\_wTilde\_X} and
\texttt{swapU\_conj\_wTilde\_Z} being
$V \tilde{W}^e(\tilde{u}) V^\dagger = \Id \ot \tau^W(e \tilde{u})$ exactly. The two signs
cancel there because the sign the first factor carries is the very phase the ancilla factor's
conjugation produces (\texttt{uOf\_conj\_wX}, \texttt{uOf\_conj\_wZ}).

The estimate rests on an identity about the Weyl system with no measurement in it:
\texttt{twirl\_mul\_twirl} is
$\bigl( \E_{\tilde{u}} \tau^X(\tilde{u}) \ot \tau^X(\tilde{u}) \bigr)
 \bigl( \E_{\tilde{v}} \tau^Z(\tilde{v}) \ot \tau^Z(\tilde{v}) \bigr)
 = \bigl( \ket{\EPR_q} \bra{\EPR_q} \bigr)^{\ot M}$. The paper proves it by an induction over the $M$ qudits, having
presented the ancilla as an iterated tensor product; here $(\C^q)^{\ot M}$ is one matrix algebra
indexed by $\F_q^M$, so it is a single entry computation and the induction disappears.

The arithmetic that turns the two near-invariances into the closeness is
\texttt{norm\_sub\_sq\_le\_of\_re\_inner\_ge}, \texttt{re\_inner\_ge\_of\_two\_close} and
\texttt{norm\_sub\_normalize\_sq\_le}, and it is the \emph{repaired} chain: the pinned paper bounded
$\norm{(H_W - \Id) v}$ by $\delta_S$ where its own preceding display gives $\delta_S$ for the
square, and compared a squared triangle inequality with $2\sqrt{\delta_S}$ where the bound on the
square is $4 \delta_S$. The conclusion $O(\sqrt{\delta_S})$ is unchanged; only intermediate
constants move. \texttt{re\_inner\_ge\_of\_two\_close} concludes exactly the paper's
$1 - 2\sqrt{\delta_S} - 2\delta_S$, and it carries a case split the paper does not: a statement with
no hidden ``for small enough $\delta_S$'' has to handle $\delta_S > 1$, where squaring
$1 - \sqrt{\delta_S}$ would reverse the inequality and where the trivial bound is already stronger.

\emph{The assembly is done.} \texttt{exists\_auxVec\_close} is item 1: a unit state on
$R \times (T \times T)$ --- the two parties' non-ancilla registers as one index, their two ancilla
halves adjacent --- that is nearly invariant under both Weyl twirls is within
$2 - 2\sqrt{1 - \eta}$ of a product $\ket{\aux} \ot \ket{\EPR_q}^{\ot M}$, with
$\eta = 2\sqrt{\delta} + 2\delta$, which is $O(\sqrt{\delta})$ as the proof above says. Two
things made it shorter than expected. The twirl is a \emph{projection}
(\texttt{twirl\_mul\_self}), not merely a contraction: the Weyl family is a homomorphism, so the
double average collapses, for each $s$ there being exactly $q^n$ pairs $(u,v)$ with $u + v = s$.
That supplies the contraction hypothesis of \texttt{norm\_sub\_sq\_le\_of\_re\_inner\_ge}
through \texttt{snorm\_le\_one\_of\_proj}, with no operator-norm estimate anywhere. And the
range of $\Id \ot \ket{\EPR}\bra{\EPR}$ consists of product vectors by inspection
(\texttt{bOp\_eprProj\_mulVec}), which is where $\ket{\aux}$ comes from --- no partial trace and
no Schmidt decomposition.

\emph{And three of item 2's displays.} Conjugation carries the exact Pauli \emph{measurement} to
the bare syndrome projector exactly, not just the observable: that is \texttt{eq:qld-unitary-6},
\texttt{swapU\_conj\_mTilde}, and at the interface
\texttt{SimulPair.swapU\_conj\_mTildeAt}. What makes it work is one mechanism, recorded on its
own because the observable's proof did not need it: the twisted commutation relation says
conjugation multiplies each Weyl operator by a character, and on the \emph{projectors} that is a
\emph{shift} --- a spectral projector is the Fourier average against a character, and two
characters compose by adding their labels (\texttt{conj\_proj\_of\_sign}), so a syndrome
projector's outcome moves by the shift's own pairing with the probe
(\texttt{conj\_syn\_of\_sign}). In $\tilde{M}^{W,u}_a$ the outcome carried at the pair outcome
$p$ is $\coded(g_{\pi p}) \cdot u + a$ and the shift is $\coded(g_{\pi p})$, so the two cancel in
characteristic two and the pair outcome disappears. That cancellation is the paper's relabelling
$h' = h + \coded(g_W)$, the passage its \texttt{cnote} records as repaired; what makes it exact
here is that the shift and the outcome are written with the same $\coded(g_{\pi p}) \cdot u$ by
construction.

The endgame's opening expansion is \texttt{sum\_snorm\_sq\_sub\_eq\_two\_sub}
(\texttt{eq:qld-unitary-7}): the two families compared there act on the \emph{same} party, so the
bipartite \texttt{one\_sub\_sum\_bornProb\_eq} does not apply, but the same three-term
expansion does. And its one estimate, \texttt{eq:qld-unitary-8}, is Schwartz--Zippel in the
packaging \texttt{eq:qld-pulling-12} already used
(\texttt{sum\_uniform\_agree\_bornProb\_le}): the index is a pair of Pauli outcomes and the
diagonal is excluded by the weight rather than by the index set, which is why
\texttt{sum\_uniform\_agree\_mass\_le} now asks for the two polynomials to be distinct only
where the weight is nonzero.

\emph{Not formalized}: the two near-invariances themselves, which are
Lemma~\ref{lem:qld-pauli-selfcons} and are hypotheses of \texttt{exists\_auxVec\_close} rather
than conclusions; and, in item 2, the assembly --- \texttt{eq:qld-unitary-7} through
\texttt{-9} and the transport above, threaded into the statement about
$V M^{(\pauli,W)}_h V^\dagger$. Hence no
\texttt{leanok} mark at either level. 

\emph{And \texttt{eq:qld-unitary-5}.} It needed no new estimate: its engine is the paper's
\texttt{fact:triangle-for-simeq}, already in Foundations as \texttt{agreeSum\_triangle} (with
$11\delta$ for the paper's $9\delta$, as Lemma~\ref{lem:qld-global-success} records). Two
adapters put it to work. \texttt{inconsistency\_triangle} is the estimate in the vocabulary this
interface states everything in --- an inconsistency rather than an agreement, the two being the
same number. And \texttt{inconsistency\_regroupVec} settles the one real obstacle, that the three
legs are not read along the same cut: $\tilde{M}^{W,u}$ needs the ancilla half with the first
party, which is the regrouped cut, while the strategy's own measurements are stated along the
padded state's. For operators that ignore the register the regrouping moves --- which the
strategy's measurements do, being extended by the identity there --- the two readings are the same
number, the regrouping being a reindexing of the whole space. \texttt{SimulPair
.inconsistency\_mTilde\_pauli\_le} is then the display, with the two middle legs --- the game's
point--point and point--Pauli consistencies, the latter read through
\texttt{pauliAtPOVM} --- as hypotheses, in the same way
\texttt{exists\_auxVec\_close} takes item 1's near-invariances. They are asked for in the form
the game supplies them: \texttt{inconsistency\_padded} reads a consistency between the strategy's
own measurements on the padded state and on the strategy's own state alike, both being extended by
the identity on the expansion's ancillas and on the padding, so
\texttt{inconsistency\_mTilde\_pauli\_le'} takes them on $\ket{\psi}$, where
\texttt{agree\_subtest\_le} leaves them.

\emph{And those two legs are discharged.} They are items 1 and 3 of Lemma~\ref{lem:qld-win} at the
point and Pauli types --- \texttt{item\_consistency} and \texttt{item\_pauli\_consistency}, both
already in --- and three steps carry each into the form the triangle wants. The point question a
content asks is the point the content carries, so the average over contents of a function of that
point is the uniform average over points (\texttt{sum\_content\_pt}); and for \emph{projective}
families a cross-party deviation is exactly twice the inconsistency
(\texttt{inconsistency\_eq\_half\_xPovmDist} --- only $\leq$ holds for general POVMs, and what
makes it an equality is that both families' masses are exactly one). That gives
\texttt{inconsistency\_pt\_pt\_le} and \texttt{inconsistency\_pt\_pauli\_le} at $86\eps$,
which is \texttt{agree\_subtest\_le}'s $172\eps$ halved, and
\texttt{inconsistency\_mTilde\_pauli\_le\_of\_win} is then \texttt{eq:qld-unitary-5} from the
game's soundness and item 1 of Lemma~\ref{lem:qld-exact-paulis} alone.

\emph{And the endgame's transport.} \texttt{abs\_qform\_sub\_qform\_le}: moving an expectation
from the product state to the padded one costs twice the operator's bound times the distance
between them. Item 1 bounds the \emph{squared} distance, which is exactly where the fourth root
in $\delta_{qld}$ comes from.
\texttt{reports/qld-stage5-blueprint-repairs.md} records the repairs above, including the
ledger's declared dependencies for node $1.2.2.16$, which name $1.2.2.4$
(Lemma~\ref{lem:qld-win}) and which the \texttt{uses} list did not.

\subsection{Undecidability of the halting problem}
\label{sec:rr-halting}

\effortMathlib

\begin{theorem}[Turing]
\label{thm:halting-undecidable}
\uses{def:re}
\lean{MIPRE.halting_undecidable, MIPRE.halting_re, MIPRE.Halting.exists_code_halts_of_isRE}
\leanok
The halting problem is r.e.\ but not decidable; it is $\RE$-complete under many-one
(indeed polynomial-time) reductions.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
\leanok
Mathlib: \texttt{ComputablePred.halting\_problem} and \texttt{halting\_problem\_re}, both
at the empty input; the many-one reduction of an r.e.\ language to halting on the empty input
is \texttt{MIPRE.IsRE.exists\_code\_halts}, by Mathlib's $S^m_n$ function
\texttt{Nat.Partrec.Code.curry}. Polynomial-time computability of the reduction is not
formalized (Section~\ref{sec:departures}).
\end{proof}

\paragraph{Source.} Turing (1936); in Mathlib as part of the
\texttt{Computability} library.

\paragraph{Comments.} Listed to close the dependency graph: combined with the halting
reduction (Theorem~\ref{thm:halting}) it yields undecidability of approximating the
game value, and $\RE$-completeness gives $\RE \subseteq \MIPstar$.

\subsection{Cook--Levin and succinct satisfiability}
\label{sec:rr-cook-levin}

\effortHard

\begin{remark}[What stage 1.2 leaves open, and where its weight is]
\label{rem:qld-admitted}
\uses{thm:qld,lem:ms-direct-anticomm,thm:lidt-cl-soundness,def:distance}
Stage $1.2$ of the ledger is by far its heaviest: after the refinement rounds of September
2026 the earlier snapshot held $149$ of $316$ live nodes and $169$ of $309$ challenges
were raised against its original $62$, against $46$ for answer reduction and $19$ for
repetition. At the September 20 pin, stage $1.2$ has $150$ active nodes
inside the $347$-node active ledger. Three facts about it bear on how this
section should be formalized.

First, the Magic Square input is no longer an assumption. It was: node $1.2.2.4.1$ was an
import of~\cite[Theorem 6.9]{CS17}, neither vendored nor reproved, carrying two adaptations
made in the paper --- a weakening of trace-norm $O(\eps)$ closeness to Euclidean
$O(\sqrt{\eps})$, and a local basis change between two anticommuting pairs. What made it
tractable was how narrowly it was consumed, through a single anticommutation consequence,
and that observation is what eventually discharged it: the consequence is now proved
directly from the game, as Lemma~\ref{lem:ms-direct-anticomm}. The completeness leg never
used it at all, resting instead on the in-file argument that anticommuting observables give
a perfect Magic Square strategy (Lemma~\ref{lem:mermin-peres} below is that strategy, and it
is already in Lean).

Second, the soundness proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:qld} is not in the main text at all: it is
deferred to a five-file appendix, which is where most of stage $1.2$'s challenge weight
sits, including one of the campaign's three \emph{critical} findings, at node $1.2.2.15$:
the construction of exact Pauli operators applied Schwartz--Zippel in the wrong direction
--- distinct polynomials of degree $md$ \emph{agree} on at most an $md/q$ fraction, so for a
non-multilinear $g$ the disagreement indicator has expectation near $1$ --- which made the
step equivalent to an unproven assertion about how much mass the simultaneous measurement
places on non-multilinear outcomes. It is resolved, by bounding that mass first. A
formalization should treat the appendix as the real content and the main-text statement as
its interface.

The other two critical findings are worth naming here, because neither reaches this
blueprint and the reason is the same in both cases. At node $1.4$, the anchored repetition
theorem was applied to a game that is not of the anchored product form at all: detyping
destroys the product structure, and restricting a repeated strategy to the event that every
coordinate is a real question costs $16^k$. At node $1.5$, the compression statement had
omitted the doubly exponential term in its entanglement lower bound, which the recursion
needs. Both are resolved upstream; both live in the entanglement-form apparatus that
Theorem~\ref{thm:direct-repetition-q} and the $\valstar$ chain replace
(Remark~\ref{rem:admitted-nodes}). All three criticals are resolved, so this is not a
soundness worry --- it is an indication of where the delicate machinery is, and two thirds
of it is machinery this route does not carry.

Third, the introspection statement that consumes all of this (node $1.2$,
Theorem~\ref{thm:introspection}) leaves a soundness gap of $1/\poly(n)$ --- \emph{not} a
constant, which is the stronger reading and the one the ledger is explicit about. That is
exactly why parallel repetition has to follow it: repetition is what turns a gap shrinking
with $n$ back into the constant $\frac12$ the recursion needs.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[What is still admitted, after the discharges of September 2026]
\label{rem:admitted-nodes}
\uses{lem:ms-direct-anticomm,lem:self-dual-basis,thm:direct-repetition-q,rem:direct-vs-anchored}
At the September 20 source pin, the ledger has $347$ active nodes:
$344$ are validated and exactly three are \emph{admitted} --- asserted on the strength of a
cited source rather than proved. The interesting fact is that these are not the three that
were admitted when this section was first written: all of those were discharged, and two of
the present three are in new territory.

What was discharged, and how, is the useful part:

\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{Anchored parallel repetition} (node $1.4.2$, the theorem of~\cite{BVY17}, which
  this blueprint no longer states) is no longer admitted upstream either: the campaign
  decomposed it into a $60$-node subtree, and the quantum relative-entropy data-processing
  inequality it rested on is derived there rather than imported --- by joint convexity plus a
  Weyl twirl realising the partial trace as a convex combination of unitary conjugations, which
  is legitimate for the finite-dimensional partial-trace case. It is \emph{unused} here
  regardless, since direct repetition (Theorem~\ref{thm:direct-repetition-q}) replaces it and
  takes a value hypothesis rather than an entanglement one
  (Remark~\ref{rem:direct-vs-anchored}).
\item \textbf{Magic Square rigidity} (node $1.2.2.4.1$) is no longer admitted either, and
  this one matters, because it was the assumption the pipeline actually consumed. The
  consequence the proof needs is not full self-testing but a single anticommutation bound,
  and that bound is now proved directly: Lemma~\ref{lem:ms-direct-anticomm}.
  Theorem~\ref{thm:ms-rigidity} stays in this section as cited background, with no live
  invocation, and the completeness leg never used it at all
  (Lemma~\ref{lem:mermin-peres}).
\item \textbf{Efficient self-dual normal bases} (node $1.1.6.1$,
  Lemma~\ref{lem:self-dual-basis}) was narrowed to one imported construction in the
  companion ledger. Normal-basis construction and self-dualization were proved over
  sixteen sub-nodes; node $1.1.6.1.1$ retained the deterministic construction of
  irreducible binary polynomials. That ledger history is unchanged. The Lean project
  now also proves this construction, with the original uniform polynomial-time contract
  (Lemma~\ref{lem:binary-uniform-irreducible}).
\end{enumerate}

Thus the paper ledger's remaining admitted input to this route is the classical
deterministic polynomial-time construction of irreducible polynomials over $\F_2$.
This is a statement about the paper's proof dependencies, not the current Lean frontier:
the simultaneous seeded CL extension and unfinished Pauli steps still require
formalization; the single-codeword seeded theorem is already proved. The mechanical ledger figure is in
\texttt{scripts/ledger-sync.py}, which walks declared dependencies as well as decomposition
and reports node $1.1.6.1.1$ as reached from $24$ of this blueprint's statements.

The other two admitted nodes sit in the downstream chapter rather than the pipeline: node
$1.8.3$ (decidability of the first-order theory of real closed fields) and node $1.8.6.1$
(the forward implication from the Connes embedding property to $\min = \max$ for
$C^*(F_\infty)$). They are reached only through Chapter~\ref{ch:downstream}, and
Remark~\ref{rem:downstream-nodes} says what they carry there.
\end{remark}

\begin{definition}[Succinctly described 3SAT and decoupled 5SAT]
\label{def:succinct-3sat}
\lean{MIPRE.SAT.Circuit.formula3, MIPRE.SAT.Circuit.formula5, MIPRE.SAT.Circuit.DescribesDecider}
\leanok
A Boolean circuit $C$ with three inputs of length $m$ and three single-bit inputs
\emph{succinctly describes} the 3SAT formula on the $2^m$ variables $x_0, \ldots,
x_{2^m - 1}$ whose clauses are the $x_{i_1}^{o_1} \vee x_{i_2}^{o_2} \vee x_{i_3}^{o_3}$
with $C(i_1, i_2, i_3, o_1, o_2, o_3) = 1$, integers presented to the circuit in binary
(\cite[Definition of succinct descriptions of 3SAT formulas, Section 10]{JNVWY20}). A
\emph{decoupled 5SAT} formula has five blocks of variables and clauses with one literal from
each block, evaluated on five separate assignments; a circuit with two inputs of length
$\ell$, three of length $r$ and five single-bit inputs succinctly describes one in the same
way. Such a circuit \emph{describes the decider $\decider$ on $(n, x, y)$ at time $T$} if,
for all $a, b \in \{0,1\}^{2^\ell}$, some three assignments complete $(a, b)$ to a
satisfying tuple exactly when $a$ and $b$ are the two-bit tape encodings ($0 \mapsto 00$,
$1 \mapsto 01$, blank $\mapsto 10$) of strings of length at most $T$ on which $\decider$
accepts $(n, x, y, \cdot, \cdot)$ within $T$ steps. Circuits have fan-in and fan-out at most
two and a terminal output gate, and their size is the number of gates.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Explicit succinct Cook--Levin]
\label{thm:succinct-sat}
\ledgernode{1.3.1}
\uses{def:succinct-3sat,def:decider,lem:correct-tableau}
\lean{MIPRE.SAT.SuccinctCookLevin, MIPRE.SAT.succinctCookLevin}
\leanok
There is a polynomial-time algorithm that, given a decider $\decider$, an index $n$, a
time bound $T$ (in binary), a question length $Q$, a bound $\sigma$ and strings $x, y$, with
$\max\{Q, 2\lceil \log n \rceil\} \leq T$, $\abs{\decider} \leq \sigma$ and $\abs{x},
\abs{y} \leq Q$, outputs a circuit $C$ on $3m + 3$ inputs which succinctly describes
(Definition~\ref{def:succinct-3sat}) a 3SAT formula $\varphi$ on $2^m$ variables such that
\begin{enumerate}
\item for all $a, b \in \{0,1\}^{2T}$, the formula $\varphi$ has a satisfying assignment
  whose first $4T$ variables are $(a, b)$ if and only if $a$ and $b$ are the two-bit tape
  encodings of strings $a', b'$ of length at most $T$ such that $\decider$ accepts $(n, x,
  y, a', b')$ within $T$ steps;
\item $m = O(\log T + \log \sigma)$ and $2^m \geq 4T$;
\item $C$ has at most $s(n, T, Q, \sigma) = \poly(\log n, \log T, Q, \sigma)$ gates;
\item the algorithm runs in time $\poly(\log n, \log T, Q, \sigma)$;
\item $m$ and $s$ are computable from the parameters in time polynomial in their bit
  lengths.
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
\leanok
\uses{lem:correct-tableau,def:succinct-3sat}
Fix one interpreter machine $U$, a multi-input Turing machine that runs the small-step
semantics of the ambient model on a serialized configuration, and put the five parameters
$n, x, y$ and the two answers on its input tapes, the answers on the two tapes left free.
Then the decider is *data* on an input tape and never becomes a machine, so the local check
circuit of $U$ is a constant; and the answer blocks are the contents of two tapes at time
$0$, which is item 1's block structure for free. The time bound $T$ in the cost of the
ambient model becomes a tableau length $S = 2^e$ with $e = 5\lceil \log T \rceil +
5 \lceil \log \sigma \rceil + 24$, because a run of the decider of cost $T$ is a run of
$U$ of at most $400000 (T + \sigma + 1)^5$ steps.

Apply Lemma~\ref{lem:correct-tableau} to $U$ at that length. Index its variables by bit
strings of width $m = 11 + 15 W + Q_b + G_b$, where $W = e + 4$ is the width of a time, a
position or a window offset: the top bit says whether the index is structured or is one of
the first $4T$ answer indices, and the remaining fields are a kind tag, a time, a tape, a
position, a value, a state, the thirteen window offsets and a gate number. Each clause
family of the tableau is then a predicate on those decoded fields, and each such predicate
is computed by a formula over the $3m + 3$ input bits of the describer, built from equality
and comparison with a constant, equality of two fields, and a ripple-carry increment. The
describer is the flattening of the disjunction of those formulas to a circuit, together with
two clauses forcing the cell at position $T + 3$ of each free tape to hold the blank, which
is what bounds the answers read off a satisfying assignment by $T$ rather than by $2S$.

Item 1 is the correctness of the tableau in both directions, transported along the indexing:
an assignment of the tableau gives one of the formula by decoding, and an assignment of the
formula gives one of the tableau by the canonical index, where an answer cell of a free tape
inside the answer block is sent to its answer index, so that the extension hypothesis on the
first $4T$ variables is exactly the time-$0$ row of that tape. Item 2 is arithmetic on $m$
and $e$. For items 3 and 4, every builder of the formula is a polynomial-time function of the
parameters, since the encoding of a formula is the encoding of its post-order list and each
builder is therefore a concatenation, a map or a fold over lists, with the widths and offsets
carried in unary; and then item 3 needs no separate argument, because the output of a
polynomial-time function is no larger than its running time and a circuit has at most as
many gates as its encoding has nodes. Item 5 follows by rounding $s$ up to a power of two
whose exponent is a multiple of the bit lengths of the parameters.
\end{proof}

\paragraph{Lean statement.} The theorem is the structure
\texttt{MIPRE.SAT.SuccinctCookLevin}: the algorithm a polynomial-time function of the
ambient model (\texttt{PolyTimeFun}) from $((\decider, n, T, Q, \sigma), x, y)$ to circuits
as data, ``within $T$ steps'' read as cost at most $T$ in the ambient model, the reading of
every time bound of the pipeline, and the five items as fields. An earlier statement asked
for a succinct description in the sense of Definition~\ref{def:succinct}, which is a
program answering bit queries and not the circuit the proof produces and the PCP consumes,
and it carried none of the five items, all of which Lemma~\ref{lem:decoupled-5sat} and the
PCP parameters depend on. The statement is validated by its consumer
(\texttt{MIPRE.SAT.DecoupledDescriber}, Lemma~\ref{lem:decoupled-5sat}) before its proof;
the route to the proof is \texttt{planning/succinct-cook-levin.md}.

\paragraph{Formalization status.} Complete: statement and proof, the structure inhabited by
\texttt{MIPRE.SAT.succinctCookLevin} (\texttt{MIPRE/TM/CookLevin/}). Three things the
formalization settled against the first design, all recorded in
\texttt{planning/succinct-cook-levin.md}. The index width has $W = e + 4$, not $e + 2$,
because a position field must hold $2S + 7$; the state field is $\lceil \log |Q| \rceil$
bits rather than a fixed four. Two clauses beyond the tableau are needed: soundness needs
answers of length at most $T$, and the tableau alone admits answers of length up to $2S$.
And the canonical index of an answer cell of a free tape is its answer index, not its
structured index --- that is what ties the extension hypothesis on the first $4T$ variables
to the time-$0$ row of the tape, and getting it wrong makes the soundness direction
unprovable rather than merely awkward.

Two notes on the Lean, both about scale. The check circuit of the interpreter machine is the
truth-table circuit of a fixed Boolean function on the window variables, so its gate count is
$7 \cdot 2^{|\mathrm{window}|}$ --- an enormous constant, exactly as the paper's
\texttt{lem:pack-check-size} carries one. Nothing unfolds it, and nothing may: the defining
terms of the state count and of the check circuit mention a cardinality and a truth table
whose evaluation does not terminate in practice, so they are kept opaque (the state width is
\texttt{irreducible} and the circuit is taken from an existential). And the program layer is
written so that each of the fifty-odd family formulas costs one \texttt{simp}: a field record
is a record of \emph{readers} of the describer's input whose evaluation is a structure literal
of those programs' own values, which makes every formula match its program definitionally.

\paragraph{Source.} Cook~\cite{Coo71} and Levin for the tableau reduction; Papadimitriou
and Yannakakis~\cite{PY86} for succinct representations and their complexity;
the explicit form needed here is~\cite[Section 10]{JNVWY20}.

\paragraph{Comments.} This is the classical core of answer reduction: the players prove
satisfiability of $\varphi$ (arithmetized over a low-degree extension) instead of
sending $(a,b)$. The statement is long but entirely combinatorial. Cook--Levin has been
formalized in other proof assistants; a Lean formalization with the explicit-uniformity
guarantees above is a substantial but well-specified sub-project.

A caution on the construction, from the verification campaign~\cite{Audit26} and not
checked here: in the Tseitin encoding of~\cite{NW19}, which the explicit form
of~\cite[Section 10]{JNVWY20} follows, the formula is reported to omit the output-wire
conjunct, so that it is satisfiable for every input and the central equivalence of the
construction fails as written. The statement above is end-to-end and so does not inherit
the defect textually, but a formalization of it must supply what the repair needs: the
output-wire conjunct, the out-degree-zero hypothesis that yields individual degree $6$,
and the exact variable count on which the later PCP parameters depend --- none of which
the statement currently fixes.

\begin{lemma}[Correctness of the Cook--Levin tableau]
\label{lem:correct-tableau}
\uses{def:multiinput-tm}
\lean{MIPRE.TM.CookLevin.tableau_sat_iff}
\leanok
Let $M$ be a multi-input Turing machine (Definition~\ref{def:multiinput-tm}) with $i$ input
tapes and $w$ work tapes over a finite alphabet, $\mathsf{acc}$, $s_0$, $s_1$ symbols and
$S$ a step bound, and fix the strings on some of the input tapes. The \emph{tableau formula}
of $S$ steps of $M$ is the 3SAT formula on the variables ``cell $p$ of tape $d$ at time $t$
holds $v$'' (one for each of $2S + 7$ cells and each value: a symbol, the blank or the
boundary marker), ``the head of tape $d$ is on cell $p$ at time $t$'', ``the state at time
$t$ is $q$'', the emission flags of every step, and, for every step and every choice of a
five-cell window on each tape, the gate variables of a circuit deciding local consistency
of the window; its clauses are the start row, the boundary rows, the free-tape row (a
string over $\{s_0, s_1\}$ followed by blanks), the emission bookkeeping, the acceptance
row and the circuit-to-3SAT clauses of the local check at every window. The formula is
satisfiable if and only if $M$, started on the fixed strings and on some strings over
$\{s_0, s_1\}$ on the free input tapes, halts within $S$ steps having output exactly
$\mathsf{acc}$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
\leanok
Completeness: the encoding of the run satisfies every clause, the window clauses because
the windows of a run pass the local check (\texttt{locallyConsistent\_localCfgOf}) and the
circuit computes it, which is its specification (\texttt{IsCheckCircuit}). Soundness: the
time-$0$ rows of a satisfying assignment determine the free strings; by induction on time
the rows encode the run, because a locally consistent window whose time-$t$ part is that
of a configuration has the time-$(t + 1)$ part of its successor (\texttt{post\_of\_pre});
the acceptance row and the emission bookkeeping then give the output. Every head stays on
an interior cell: a head moves at most one cell per step and a tape has $2S + 7$ cells.
\end{proof}

\paragraph{Source.} The tableau of~\cite[Section 10]{JNVWY20} and the standard argument of
Cook~\cite{Coo71}; the companion paper's \texttt{answer\_reduction.tex} (its
\texttt{lem:correct-tableau}).

\paragraph{Comments.} The lemma is Cook--Levin for one fixed machine; the theorem applies
it to a fixed interpreter machine for the decider's program, and the succinct description
is a circuit for the clauses of this formula --- pieces S2 and S3 of
\texttt{planning/succinct-cook-levin.md}. The interpreter machine (S2) is formalized:
\texttt{MIPRE.TM.Interp.U}, with \texttt{accepts\_of\_acceptsWithin} and
\texttt{acceptsWithin\_of\_accepts} (\texttt{MIPRE/TM/Interp/Run.lean}) --- on the inputs
$(\decider, n, T, x, y, a, b)$ with $\abs{a}, \abs{b} \leq T$, it halts having output
exactly $1$, within an explicit polynomial number of steps, if and only if $\decider$
accepts $(n, x, y, a, b)$ within cost $T$. The local check circuit is a parameter of the
formula, with its specification a hypothesis of the lemma, so that the lemma is proved once
and the circuit supplied with the describer. The windows are five cells wide: a head at one
of the middle three cells then sees, in the window, both the cell it leaves and the cell it
reaches, and the clamped moves of an input head at the ends of the input are decided from
the window alone.

\subsection{Self-dual normal bases}
\label{sec:rr-self-dual-basis}

\effortMedium

\tnote{Is this needed?}

\begin{definition}[Self-dual and normal bases]
\label{def:self-dual-basis}
\lean{MIPRE.LowDegree.IsSelfDualBasis, MIPRE.LowDegree.IsNormalBasis}
\leanok
Let $K / \F$ be a finite extension of finite fields, $q = \abs{\F}$. A basis $(e_i)$ of $K$
over $\F$ is \emph{self-dual} when its Gram matrix for the trace form is the identity,
$\mathrm{tr}_{K / \F}(e_i e_j) = \delta_{ij}$, and \emph{normal} when its vectors are the
Frobenius orbit $\alpha, \alpha^q, \alpha^{q^2}, \ldots$ of a single element $\alpha \in K$.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Self-dual coordinates are trace pairings]
\label{lem:downsize-field}
\uses{def:self-dual-basis}
\lean{MIPRE.LowDegree.coord_eq_trace, MIPRE.LowDegree.trace_mul_eq_dot}
\leanok
Let $(e_i)$ be a self-dual basis of $K / \F$ and $\downsize(x)$ the coordinate vector of
$x \in K$. Then
\begin{enumerate}
\item $\downsize(x)_i = \mathrm{tr}_{K/\F}(x e_i)$, and
\item $\mathrm{tr}_{K/\F}(xy) = \downsize(x) \cdot \downsize(y)$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
\leanok
\uses{def:self-dual-basis}
For the first, expand $x = \sum_j \downsize(x)_j e_j$, multiply by $e_i$ and take the trace:
self-duality kills every term but $j = i$. The second follows from the first by the same
expansion, $\mathrm{tr}(xy) = \sum_j \downsize(x)_j \mathrm{tr}(e_j y) = \sum_j \downsize(x)_j
\downsize(y)_j$.
\end{proof}

\paragraph{Source.} Items 1 and 2 of the paper's \texttt{lem:downsize\_field}
(\texttt{preliminaries.tex}); ledger node \texttt{1.1.6.1.11}, which also records that neither
item needs normality or odd $k$. The paper's third item, compatibility of the downsized matrix
map with $\downsize$ ($\downsize_{\mathrm{m}}(M)\downsize(v) = \downsize(Mv)$), is not
formalized here: it is wanted by the conditionally linear downsizing of
Lemma~\ref{lem:cl-downsize}, which is stated over $\F_2$ and so has not needed it.

\paragraph{Comments.} These two identities are why the pipeline insists on self-duality rather
than on an arbitrary basis: they make $\downsize$ computable by the trace form, with no matrix
inversion, and turn the trace form into the standard inner product on coordinates. They are
also what collapses the Pauli phase index $G_E \downsize(b)$ to $\downsize(b)$ at $G_E = I$
(ledger node \texttt{1.1.6.1.13}).

\begin{lemma}[Deterministic self-dual normal basis]
\label{lem:self-dual-basis}
\uses{def:self-dual-basis}
\ledgernode{1.1.6, 1.1.6.1, 1.1.6.1.1}
\lean{MIPRE.SAT.shoupSelfDualNormalBasis_selfDual,
  MIPRE.SAT.shoupSelfDualNormalBasis_normal,
  MIPRE.SAT.shoupSelfDualNormalBasisProg,
  MIPRE.SAT.shoupSelfDualNormalBasisProg_correct,
  MIPRE.SAT.shoupSelfDualNormalDataProg,
  MIPRE.SAT.shoupSelfDualNormalDataProg_correct,
  MIPRE.SAT.shoupSelfDualNormalDataProg_time_le,
  MIPRE.SAT.effective_selfDualNormalBasis}
\leanok
There is a deterministic algorithm that, given an odd integer $k > 0$, outputs in time
$\poly(k)$ a self-dual normal basis of $\F_{2^k}$ over $\F_2$ together with its
multiplication tables.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
\leanok
Construct the normal basis by Lemma~\ref{lem:effective-normal-element}, apply
Lemma~\ref{lem:effective-normal-selfdualization}, and transport the multiplication
table by Lemma~\ref{lem:effective-field-matrices}. The composed ambient program
has unary degree as its only input and prints both the basis vectors and all
table coefficients. Its source runtime is bounded by a fixed polynomial in $k$.
\end{proof}

\paragraph{Source.} Shoup's deterministic irreducible-polynomial
construction~\cite{Sho90}, and then the companion campaign's own constructions. The
attribution here used to name Lenstra's isomorphism algorithm~\cite{Len91} and the classical
characteristic-$2$ existence results, following the discussion
in~\cite[Section 3]{JNVWY20}; the campaign has since \emph{proved} both steps --- ledger node
$1.1.6.1.6$ discharges the normal-basis construction contract ``formerly attributed to
Lenstra'' and node $1.1.6.1.8$ the self-dualization contract ``formerly attributed to Wang''
--- and the Lean development now proves the remaining Shoup construction as well.

\paragraph{Comments.} Needed so that verifiers can do field arithmetic over
$\F_{2^k}$ using only $\F_2$-data (the ``downsize'' maps), uniformly and
deterministically. This is why field sizes are restricted to $q = 2^k$, $k$ odd
(``admissible''). Self-contained algebra/algorithmics; independent of everything
quantum. Two consumers now name it: the admissible field with a bit representation that
Theorem~\ref{thm:pcp-decider} takes as a parameter (\texttt{MIPRE.SAT.BinField}), and the
downsized samplers of Lemma~\ref{lem:cl-downsize} once they move past field size $2$.

The companion ledger retains its historical admission of Shoup's construction at
node $1.1.6.1.1$. The Lean declaration
\texttt{MIPRE.LowDegree.exists\_shoup\_irreducible} is now a theorem, proved by the
explicit program of Lemma~\ref{lem:binary-uniform-irreducible}. Its input is the
requested degree in unary, so the ambient bound is polynomial in the degree and
includes printing all coefficient bits. The proof covers every positive degree
in characteristic two, including powers of two, with fixed behavior at degree zero.

The complete effective self-dual normal-basis construction in
\texttt{SAT/EffectiveNormalBasis.lean} composes this proved field constructor with
the verified normal-element, inverse-Gram self-dualization, and multiplication-table
programs. Fixed-space separation is performed directly in the cyclic algebra,
as detailed in Lemma~\ref{lem:effective-normal-element}; this avoids materializing
polynomial factors while implementing the primitive-projection argument.
Exact axiom guards in \texttt{MIPRE/Axioms.lean} check the field constructor, the
classical PCP decider, and this effective basis theorem against Lean's standard
axioms alone. This local proof does not rewrite the companion ledger's events or
discharge unrelated unfinished pipeline theorems.

\begin{lemma}[Self-dualization in the group algebra]
\label{lem:group-algebra-selfdualization}
\ledgernode{1.1.6.1.7, 1.1.6.1.8}
\uses{def:self-dual-basis}
\lean{MIPRE.LowDegree.exists_mul_involute_eq, MIPRE.LowDegree.mul_self_bijective,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.exists_mul_involute_eq_of_charTwo,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.binarySquareRoot, MIPRE.LowDegree.coeff_binarySquareRoot,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.binarySquareRoot_mul_self, MIPRE.LowDegree.binarySquareRoot_mul_involute}
\leanok
Let $R$ be a commutative ring on which $x \mapsto x^2$ is a bijection and let $\tau$ be a ring
automorphism of $R$. Then every $u \in R$ with $\tau(u) = u$ is of the form $a \cdot \tau(a)$.

Squaring \emph{is} a bijection of the group algebra $\F[G]$ when $\F$ is a finite field of
characteristic two and $G$ is a finite abelian group of odd order.
For $\F=\F_2$, its inverse has the explicit coefficient formula $b(g)=u(g+g)$.
This $b$ satisfies $b^2=u$, and $b\tau(b)=u$ whenever $\tau(u)=u$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
\leanok
For the first statement, let $b$ be the square root of $u$. Then $\tau(b)^2 = \tau(b^2) =
\tau(u) = u = b^2$, so $\tau(b) = b$ by injectivity of squaring, and $b \cdot \tau(b) = b^2 = u$.

For the second, squaring is additive in characteristic two, and on a single generator
$\delta_g r$ it is $\delta_{g + g} r^2$. Doubling is injective on $G$: if $(a - b) + (a - b) =
0$ then the additive order of $a - b$ divides both $2$ and $\abs{G}$, which are coprime because
$\abs{G}$ is odd. So the coefficient of $x^2$ at $g + g$ is the square of the coefficient of $x$
at $g$, and $x^2 = 0$ forces $x = 0$. Squaring is then injective, because $(x - y)^2 = x^2 -
y^2$ in characteristic two, and injective implies bijective on a finite set.
Over $\F_2$ the coefficients are fixed by squaring, so the displayed coefficient
permutation gives the square root directly. The following lemma implements that
permutation in the ambient cost model.
\end{proof}

\paragraph{Comments.} This is the self-dualization step of Lemma~\ref{lem:self-dual-basis}, and
it is where oddness of $k$ is used. If $\alpha$ is a normal element of $\F_{2^k}/\F_2$ with
Galois group $G$, the trace-form Gram matrix of its orbit is the circulant determined by
$c \in \F_2[G]$, $c(g) = \mathrm{tr}(\alpha \cdot g\alpha)$: it is a unit, because the Gram
matrix of a basis under the nondegenerate trace form is invertible, and it is fixed by the
involution $\tau$ induced by $g \mapsto g^{-1}$, because that Gram matrix is symmetric.
Replacing $\alpha$ by $a \cdot \alpha$ replaces $c$ by $a \cdot \tau(a) \cdot c$, so a self-dual
normal basis is exactly a solution of $a \cdot \tau(a) = c^{-1}$, which is what the lemma
supplies. Nodes $1.1.6.1.7$ and $1.1.6.1.8$ carry an explicit circulant Gram manipulation
instead; the argument above replaces it, and needs no factorization of $X^k - 1$ and no norm
computation in the factors.

What the lemma does \emph{not} do is assemble a basis: the group algebra must be made to act on
$\F_{2^k}$, the Gram identity must be proved, and $c$ must be shown to be a unit. Those three
are bookkeeping, and Lemma~\ref{lem:self-dual-basis-exists} now carries them out; the algorithm
is a separate matter again.

\begin{lemma}[The binary square-root program]
\label{lem:binary-square-root-program}
\ledgernode{1.1.6.1.7,1.1.6.1.8}
\uses{lem:group-algebra-selfdualization}
\lean{MIPRE.LowDegree.rootBitsProg, MIPRE.LowDegree.rootBitsProg_time_le,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.groupOfBits_rootBits, MIPRE.LowDegree.rootBitsProg_square}
\leanok
For odd $k$, the square-root permutation on the coefficient vector of
$\F_2[\mathbb{Z}/k\mathbb{Z}]$ is computed by one uniform ambient program in
time polynomial in $k$. The output vector has length $k$ and represents a
square root of the input group-algebra element.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
\leanok
Split the list into coefficients in even positions and coefficients in odd
positions, then append those lists. At position $i$ this reads the coefficient
at $2i$ modulo $k$. A size-bounded fold implements the split. The input encoding
has size at most $4k+1$, giving the stated polynomial bound. The preceding
lemma supplies the algebraic identity.
\end{proof}
\paragraph{Scope.} This completes the square-root operation only. Computing
the normal element, the inverse Gram element and the transported field tables
remains part of Lemma~\ref{lem:self-dual-basis}.

\begin{lemma}[Existence of a self-dual normal basis]
\label{lem:self-dual-basis-exists}
\uses{def:self-dual-basis,lem:group-algebra-selfdualization}
\lean{MIPRE.LowDegree.gramPair, MIPRE.LowDegree.gramPair_ofAlg,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.isUnit_gram, MIPRE.LowDegree.exists_gramPair_eq_one,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.exists_isSelfDualBasis_isNormalBasis,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.exists_selfDualNormalBasis_two}
\leanok
Let $K / \F$ be a Galois extension of finite fields of characteristic two whose degree is odd.
Then $K$ has a basis over $\F$ that is at once normal and self-dual. In particular
$\F_{2^k}$ has a self-dual normal basis over $\F_2$ for every odd $k \geq 1$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
\leanok
\uses{lem:group-algebra-selfdualization}
Let $G = \mathrm{Gal}(K/\F)$, which is cyclic, hence abelian, so $\F[G]$ is a commutative ring, and
$\abs{G} = [K : \F]$ is odd. Let $\alpha$ be the normal element Mathlib's normal basis theorem
supplies, write $\mathrm{ofAlg}(a)$ for the element of $K$ with normal-basis coordinates
$a \in \F[G]$, and let
\[
  c(x, y) \in \F[G], \qquad c(x, y)_g = \mathrm{tr}(x \cdot g y), \qquad c = c(\alpha, \alpha).
\]
Because $\mathrm{tr} \circ g = \mathrm{tr}$, the pairing of two members of one orbit depends
only on their ratio, and expanding both coordinate sums gives the transformation law
\[
  c(\mathrm{ofAlg}(a), \mathrm{ofAlg}(b)) = a \cdot \tau(b) \cdot c,
\]
where $\tau$ is the involution of $\F[G]$ induced by $g \mapsto g^{-1}$. The element $c$ is
fixed by $\tau$, because $\mathrm{tr}(\alpha \cdot g^{-1}\alpha) =
\mathrm{tr}(g\alpha \cdot \alpha)$, and it is a unit: if $c \cdot z = 0$ then the
transformation law with $b = \tau(z)$ makes $\mathrm{tr}(\mathrm{ofAlg}(\tau z) \cdot x)$
vanish for every $x \in K$, so $\mathrm{ofAlg}(\tau z) = 0$ by nondegeneracy of the trace form,
so $z = 0$; a non-zero-divisor in a finite commutative ring is a unit. Then $c^{-1}$ is also
$\tau$-fixed, and Lemma~\ref{lem:group-algebra-selfdualization} --- squaring is bijective on
$\F[G]$ in characteristic two with $\abs{G}$ odd --- gives $a$ with $a \cdot \tau(a) = c^{-1}$.
For $\beta = \mathrm{ofAlg}(a)$ the transformation law reads $c(\beta, \beta) = 1$, which says
exactly that the Frobenius orbit of $\beta$ is orthonormal for the trace form. It is in
particular linearly independent, so it is a basis, and it is normal by construction.
\end{proof}

\paragraph{Comments.} This is existence, not construction, so it is not
Lemma~\ref{lem:self-dual-basis} and does not discharge any ledger node: the normal element
comes from Mathlib's \texttt{IsGalois.normalBasis}, which is a theorem and not an algorithm,
and nothing here is claimed to run in $\poly(k)$. What it does settle is that the three
bookkeeping items left open by Lemma~\ref{lem:group-algebra-selfdualization} --- making the
group algebra act, the Gram identity, and unitness of $c$ --- are genuinely bookkeeping, and
that the short self-dualization argument assembles. The formalization indexes the group algebra
by $G$ itself rather than by $\mathbb{Z}/k$, which is what makes $\mathrm{ofAlg}$ the inverse of
Mathlib's \texttt{normalBasis.repr} on the nose; the \texttt{CommGroup} instance comes from
cyclicity, supplied locally.

\begin{lemma}[Effective normalization of binary polynomials]
\label{lem:binary-polynomial-normalization}
\lean{MIPRE.LowDegree.BinaryPolynomial.normalizeBitsProg,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.BinaryPolynomial.normalizeBitsProg_apply,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.BinaryPolynomial.length_normalizeBits_le,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.BinaryPolynomial.evalBits_normalizeBits,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.BinaryPolynomial.polyOfBits_normalizeBits,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.BinaryPolynomial.normalizeBits_getLast}
\leanok
One fixed deterministic ambient program removes the trailing zero coefficients
from any little-endian binary coefficient list in polynomial time. Its output
is no longer than the input, is empty or ends in one, and represents the same
polynomial over $\F_2$. More generally it preserves Horner evaluation at any
element of any commutative ring. This program requires no irreducible-polynomial
construction.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
\leanok
Fold the coefficient list from its highest coefficient downwards. Ignore a zero
while the accumulator is empty; otherwise prepend the current coefficient.
Induction proves preservation of Horner evaluation and the leading-coefficient
property. Each step adds at most one coefficient, so every intermediate encoded
accumulator has size bounded by the input and the generic bounded-fold program
gives the polynomial runtime bound.
\end{proof}
\paragraph{Scope.} This generic normalization result is used by the complete
irreducible-polynomial constructor in Lemma~\ref{lem:binary-uniform-irreducible}.

\begin{lemma}[Effective monic binary division]
\label{lem:binary-monic-division}
\uses{lem:binary-polynomial-normalization}
\lean{MIPRE.LowDegree.BinaryPolynomial.divModBitsProg,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.BinaryPolynomial.divModBitsProg_apply,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.BinaryPolynomial.divModBits_width,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.BinaryPolynomial.polyOfBits_divModBits,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.BinaryPolynomial.degree_divModBits_remainder_lt}
\leanok
Given a binary coefficient list $a$ and the $d$ lower coefficients $p$ of a
monic polynomial $f$, one deterministic ambient polynomial-time program returns
coefficient lists $q,r$ satisfying $fq+r=a$ and $\deg r<d$. Their lengths are
at most the length of $a$ and $d$, respectively. The case $d=0$ divides by one
and returns zero remainder.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
\leanok
Process the dividend by Horner's rule, keeping a remainder of width $d$ and
recording its discarded high bit as the next quotient coefficient. Each shift
subtracts the indicated multiple of the monic divisor; induction gives the
reconstruction identity. The remainder width bounds its degree. Normalize both
outputs. The quotient grows by one bit per iteration and the remainder cannot
grow, even from malformed initial states, so the ambient bounded-fold theorem
gives the global polynomial bound.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Canonical coordinates in monic binary quotients]
\label{lem:binary-monic-quotient}
\lean{MIPRE.LowDegree.BinaryQuotient.length_toBits,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.BinaryQuotient.ofBits_toBits,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.BinaryQuotient.toBits_ofBits,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.BinaryQuotient.evalBits_eq_iff,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.BinaryQuotient.ofBits_xor,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.BinaryQuotient.ofBits_mulReduce}
\leanok
For every monic $f\in\F_2[X]$ of degree $d$, the quotient $\F_2[X]/(f)$ has
canonical $d$-bit coordinates in its power basis. Encoding and decoding are
inverse on correctly sized lists, and equality of their root evaluations is
equivalent to equality of the lists. The uniform XOR and reduced-multiplication
programs implement quotient addition and multiplication. Irreducibility and
squarefreeness are not required.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
\leanok
The monic power basis identifies each quotient element with its $d$ binary
coefficients. The basis representation identities prove the two inverse laws.
Evaluation at the canonical root equals decoding on fixed-width vectors.
The root satisfies the monic reduction equation, so the generic XOR and Horner
multiplication identities give the stated arithmetic. The constant-one modulus
has a trivial quotient and empty coordinate vectors.
\end{proof}
\paragraph{Scope.} The coordinate maps describe the semantics of the explicit
coefficient programs. They do not enumerate a quotient or supply an abstract
field conversion as an executable primitive.

\begin{lemma}[Canonical binary Euclidean algorithms]
\label{lem:binary-euclidean-algorithms}
\uses{lem:binary-monic-division}
\lean{MIPRE.LowDegree.BinaryPolynomial.normalizeBits_eq_nil_iff,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.BinaryPolynomial.length_normalizeBits,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.BinaryPolynomial.divModBits_eq_div_modByMonic,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.BinaryPolynomial.gcdBitsProg,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.BinaryPolynomial.polyOfBits_gcdBits,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.BinaryPolynomial.gcdBits_width,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.BinaryPolynomial.quotientBitsProg,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.BinaryPolynomial.polyOfBits_quotientBits,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.BinaryPolynomial.polyOfBits_quotientBits_mul,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.BinaryPolynomial.quotientBits_width}
\leanok
Normalization of a binary coefficient list is empty exactly when its polynomial
is zero; otherwise its length is the degree plus one. List-based monic division
agrees with polynomial division. Uniform deterministic polynomial-time programs
compute the normalized polynomial gcd and polynomial quotient on all binary
coefficient lists. The quotient by zero is zero. For a nonzero divisor which
divides the dividend, the quotient reconstructs the dividend by multiplication.
The gcd width is at most the larger input width, and quotient width is at most
the dividend width.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
\leanok
A nonempty normalized list ends in one, determining the degree and monicity.
The reconstruction identity and strict remainder-degree bound uniquely identify
monic quotient and remainder. Euclidean steps preserve the gcd and strictly
decrease the normalized remainder length until it is zero, so the original
divisor width bounds the iteration count. Intermediate widths never exceed the
initial maximum, including malformed states of the ambient loop. Quotient
computation normalizes the divisor and invokes monic division, with an explicit
zero branch.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Effective Frobenius fixed spaces in binary quotients]
\label{lem:binary-quotient-fixed-space}
\uses{lem:binary-monic-quotient}
\lean{MIPRE.LowDegree.BinaryQuotient.frobeniusMatrixProg,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.BinaryQuotient.frobeniusMatrixProg_correct,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.BinaryQuotient.fixedGeneratorsProg,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.BinaryQuotient.fixedGeneratorsProg_correct,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.BinaryQuotient.fixedGenerator_idempotent,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.BinaryQuotient.fixedGenerator_spans,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.BinaryQuotient.square_bijective}
\leanok
For a monic binary modulus of degree $d$, uniform deterministic polynomial-time
programs compute the matrix of squaring and a family of $d$ vectors spanning its
fixed space. Every decoded generator is idempotent, and every idempotent lies
in their binary span. If the modulus is squarefree, squaring on the quotient is
bijective.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
\leanok
Square each power-basis vector using the supplied modulus and transpose the
resulting columns. The fixed-space matrix is this matrix plus the identity;
the verified binary kernel program prints a spanning family. The power-basis
coordinate equivalence transports the kernel and its span back to quotient
elements. For a squarefree modulus, equality of squares makes the square of
the difference zero. A polynomial lift of that difference has its square
divisible by the modulus; squarefreeness implies divisibility of the lift
itself. Squaring is therefore injective, hence bijective on the finite quotient.
\end{proof}
\paragraph{Scope.} Computing a spanning family does not enumerate its binary
linear combinations. The next lemma extracts and reconstructs the factors.

\begin{lemma}[Deterministic squarefree binary factorization]
\label{lem:binary-squarefree-factorization}
\uses{lem:binary-quotient-fixed-space,lem:binary-euclidean-algorithms}
\lean{MIPRE.LowDegree.BinaryQuotient.factorBitsProg,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.BinaryQuotient.factorBitsProg_factors,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.BinaryQuotient.factorBitsProg_prod}
\leanok
A fixed deterministic polynomial-time program factors every nonconstant monic
squarefree polynomial over $\F_2$, given its lower coefficient bits. Every
printed factor is monic and irreducible, and their product is the input.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
\leanok
Start with the identity idempotent and split each retained component by each
computed Frobenius-fixed generator, discarding zero components. Orthogonality
bounds the number of nonzero components by the quotient dimension. A component
that no generator splits is primitive, since the generators span all
idempotents. For a primitive component represented by $E$, compute
$\mathrm{gcd}(f,E-1)$ with the verified Euclidean program. Divisibility by this gcd is
equivalent to annihilation by $E$ in the quotient. Bijectivity of squaring and
primitivity make the corresponding quotient a field, proving irreducibility.
Orthogonal components yield coprime factors, while their sum is the identity;
the resulting factors therefore reconstruct $f$. The executable splitting loop
caps intermediate lists at the input dimension, providing a polynomial bound
on every raw input as well as on inputs satisfying the squarefree contract.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Effective closed Frobenius orbit polynomials]
\label{lem:binary-orbit-polynomial}
\uses{lem:binary-monic-quotient}
\lean{MIPRE.LowDegree.BinaryQuotient.orbitPolynomialBitsProg,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.BinaryQuotient.map_orbitPolynomialBits,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.BinaryQuotient.orbitPolynomialBits_monic_natDegree,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.BinaryQuotient.orbitPolynomialBits_eq_minpoly,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.BinaryQuotient.orbitPolynomialBits_irreducible}
\leanok
Given a supplied monic irreducible binary modulus, a fixed-width representation
of $a$ in its quotient field, and a unary integer $r$, a fixed polynomial-time
program prints the binary coefficients of
$\prod_{i=0}^{r-1}(X+a^{2^i})$ whenever $a^{2^r}=a$.
The output polynomial is monic of degree $r$. If $r>0$ is the minimal-polynomial
degree of $a$ over $\F_2$, the output is that minimal polynomial and is irreducible.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
\leanok
Repeated modular squaring constructs the orbit, and polynomial multiplication
in the supplied quotient constructs the product. Closing the orbit makes each
coefficient fixed by squaring. In a field of characteristic two such a
coefficient is zero or one; faithful power-basis coordinates therefore justify
reading its constant bit. The resulting binary polynomial vanishes at $a$.
Minimal-polynomial divisibility, monicity, and equal degrees identify it with
the minimal polynomial when the exact-degree hypothesis holds. All loops are
bounded by supplied unary lengths and coefficient widths.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Effective odd-prime nonresidues]
\label{lem:binary-odd-prime-nonresidue}
\uses{lem:binary-squarefree-factorization}
\lean{MIPRE.LowDegree.BinaryPolynomial.nonresidueLiftBitsProg,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.BinaryPolynomial.nonresidueLiftBitsProg_correct,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.BinaryPolynomial.nonresidueLiftBits_order,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.BinaryPolynomial.nonresidueLiftBits_degree_coprime}
\leanok
For every odd prime $q$, a fixed polynomial-time program on unary $q$ prints a
canonical monic irreducible binary polynomial $f$ of degree
$m=\mathrm{ord}_q(2)$, with $\mathrm{gcd}(q,m)=1$, such that the specified root of
$f$ in $\F_2[X]/(f)$ has no $q$th root in that field.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
\leanok
Choose the first irreducible factor of the squarefree cyclotomic polynomial
$\Phi_q$. Repeatedly factor $f(X^q)$. A singleton factor list certifies that the
current root is a nonresidue; retain the current polynomial and stop. Otherwise,
every factor has the original degree $m$, and the first factor advances the
primitive-root order from $q^j$ to $q^{j+1}$. The order of a nonzero element of a
field of size $2^m$ bounds the number of advances by $m$, so $m$ iterations
suffice. Every query is squarefree by its cyclotomic invariant. Clipping each
nonterminal choice to the initial coefficient width bounds all raw executions;
the exact-degree invariant shows that clipping preserves valid executions.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Effective irreducibles of every power-of-two degree]
\label{lem:binary-power-two-irreducible}
\uses{lem:binary-orbit-polynomial}
\lean{MIPRE.LowDegree.BinaryArtinSchreier.powerTwoBitsProg,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.BinaryArtinSchreier.powerTwoBits_correct,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.BinaryArtinSchreier.powerTwoBits_length}
\leanok
A fixed polynomial-time program, given the requested degree $2^e$ in unary,
prints exactly $2^e+1$ coefficient bits of a monic irreducible polynomial of
degree $2^e$ over $\F_2$, for every $e\geq 0$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
\leanok
Degree one uses $X$, and degree two starts from $X^2+X+1$. At each later stage,
the current root $\alpha$ has the invariant that
$Y^2+Y+(\alpha^3+\alpha^2)$ is irreducible. The quadratic-basis calculation
preserves this invariant at the next root. Compute the closed orbit polynomial
of $\alpha^3+\alpha^2$ over the current field and substitute $X^2+X$; the result
is monic of twice the current degree. The new quadratic root lies outside the
current field, and the power-of-two subfield divisibility argument proves its
full doubled degree. The executable loop stops when the requested width is
reached and caps intermediate coefficients by that width. Its fuel is the
unary requested degree, so even malformed inputs satisfy one polynomial bound.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Effective irreducibles of every odd-prime-power degree]
\label{lem:binary-odd-prime-power-irreducible}
\uses{lem:binary-odd-prime-nonresidue,lem:binary-orbit-polynomial}
\lean{MIPRE.LowDegree.BinaryQuotient.traceBitsProg,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.BinaryQuotient.evalBits_traceBits,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.BinaryPrimePowerTrace.flat_trace_natDegree,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.BinaryPolynomial.oddPrimePowerBitsProg,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.BinaryPolynomial.oddPrimePowerBits_correct,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.BinaryPolynomial.oddPrimePowerBits_length,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.BinaryPolynomial.oddPrimePowerBits_width_le}
\leanok
A fixed polynomial-time program takes unary $q$ and unary $r$. If $q$ is an odd
prime and $r=q^{e+1}$ for $e\geq 0$, it prints exactly $r+1$ coefficient bits
of a monic irreducible binary polynomial of degree $r$. On arbitrary inputs its
output has at most $r+1$ bits, where $r$ denotes the second input length.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
\leanok
Construct the nonresidue polynomial $f$ of degree $m=\mathrm{ord}_q(2)$.
The odd-prime Kummer criterion makes $f(X^r)$ irreducible. In its explicitly
represented quotient, with root $\beta$, compute
$\gamma=\sum_{i=0}^{m-1}\beta^{2^{ri}}$ by repeated modular squaring and addition.
Its Frobenius period divides $r$. To show its degree is exactly $r$, use the
subfield generated by $\beta^q$: membership of $\gamma$ in that subfield would
produce a nonzero polynomial of degree less than $q$ vanishing at $\beta$.
The residues of $2^{ri}$ modulo $q$ are distinct because $\mathrm{gcd}(r,m)=1$, which gives a
nonzero coefficient in that polynomial. The relative degree of $\beta$ is $q$,
a contradiction. The closed orbit program therefore prints the minimal
polynomial of $\gamma$. The flat extension has degree $mr$, and all arithmetic,
trace, and orbit loops use the supplied unary parameters and fixed quotient
widths, giving a global polynomial bound.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Effective combination of coprime degrees]
\label{lem:binary-coprime-degree-combination}
\uses{lem:binary-monic-quotient,lem:binary-orbit-polynomial}
\lean{MIPRE.LowDegree.BinaryQuotient.composedSumBitsProg,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.BinaryQuotient.composedSumBits_correct}
\leanok
A fixed polynomial-time program combines monic irreducible binary polynomials
$f,g$ of coprime degrees $a,b$ into a monic irreducible binary polynomial of
degree $ab$. Its inputs are the lower coefficient bits of $f$ and the full
coefficient bits of $g$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
\leanok
In the supplied quotient for $f$, form
$H(X)=\prod_{i=0}^{a-1}g(X+\alpha^{2^i})$, where $\alpha$ is its specified root.
Frobenius permutes the factors, so the coefficients descend to binary bits.
This polynomial is monic of degree $ab$ and vanishes at $\alpha+\beta$ for a
root $\beta$ of $g$ in a common algebraic closure. Coprimality of the two
Frobenius periods forces the degree of $\alpha+\beta$ to be $ab$; comparison
with its minimal polynomial proves irreducibility. Polynomial translation,
multiplication, and the length-$a$ product use bounded coefficient arrays.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Effective unary prime-power decomposition]
\label{lem:binary-degree-decomposition}
\lean{MIPRE.LowDegree.DegreeArithmetic.modUnaryProg,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.DegreeArithmetic.dvdUnaryProg,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.DegreeArithmetic.mulUnaryProg,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.DegreeArithmetic.primeUnaryProg,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.DegreeArithmetic.primeUnary_eq,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.DegreeArithmetic.primePowerUnaryProg,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.DegreeArithmetic.primePowerUnary_correct,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.DegreeArithmetic.primePowerPairsProg,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.DegreeArithmetic.primePowerPairs_eq,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.DegreeArithmetic.primePowerPairs_prod,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.DegreeArithmetic.primePowerPairs_pairwise,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.BinaryDegreeFactors.degreeFactors_prod,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.BinaryDegreeFactors.degreeFactors_pairwise,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.BinaryDegreeFactors.degreeFactors_dvd,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.BinaryDegreeFactors.degreeFactors_pos,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.BinaryDegreeFactors.degreeFactors_le,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.BinaryDegreeFactors.prefix_prod_dvd}
\leanok
A fixed polynomial-time program on unary $n$ prints, in ascending base order,
the pairs $(q,q^{v_q(n)})$ for $0\leq q\leq n$, with exponent zero at nonprime
indices. For $n>0$, the second coordinates are positive, pairwise coprime, and
have product $n$. Each listed degree, and every prefix product, divides $n$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
\leanok
Enumerate the unary indices from zero through $n$, test primality by trial
division, and compute the largest dividing prime power by bounded unary
multiplication and divisibility tests. Each arithmetic routine has a polynomial
bound on all inputs. The resulting list agrees with the proof-only list
$q^{n.\mathrm{factorization}(q)}$. Unique factorization gives its product;
distinct prime indices give coprime powers, and all other indices contribute
one. Divisibility of entries and prefix products follows from the product
identity. The mathematical factorization function appears only in correctness
proofs, not in the executable program.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Uniform deterministic binary irreducible polynomials]
\label{lem:binary-uniform-irreducible}
\ledgernode{1.1.6.1.1}
\uses{lem:binary-power-two-irreducible,lem:binary-odd-prime-power-irreducible,
  lem:binary-coprime-degree-combination,lem:binary-degree-decomposition}
\lean{MIPRE.LowDegree.BinaryPolynomial.irreducibleBitsProg,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.BinaryPolynomial.irreducibleBits_correct,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.BinaryPolynomial.irreducibleBits_length,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.BinaryPolynomial.irreducibleBitsProg_time_le,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.exists_shoup_irreducible,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.shoupIrreducible,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.shoupIrreducible_monic,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.shoupIrreducible_irreducible,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.shoupIrreducible_natDegree}
\leanok
There is one fixed deterministic ambient program and one polynomial $R$ such
that, on unary input $n\geq1$, the program prints $n+1$ coefficient bits of a
monic irreducible polynomial over $\F_2$ of degree $n$ within $R(n)$ steps.
The same bound includes degree zero, where the program returns the constant
polynomial one. The public constructor \texttt{shoupIrreducible} is this
specified program, and \texttt{exists\_shoup\_irreducible} is a proved theorem.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
\leanok
Compute the unary prime-power decomposition of $n$ and dispatch each contribution
to the power-of-two or odd-prime-power constructor; neutral contributions use
$X$. Starting from $X$, fold the coprime-degree combination over this ordered
list. The degrees are pairwise coprime and have product $n$. Every intermediate
degree is at most $n$, so clipping the lower coefficients to the original unary
input length preserves each valid step and gives a global fold bound. Append
the leading coefficient to obtain the exact $n+1$-bit output. The composite
\texttt{PolyTimeFun} supplies a polynomial bound on actual encoded execution;
substituting the unary encoding size $2n+1$ gives the displayed polynomial $R$.
No existential choice is used to select the program.
\end{proof}
\paragraph{Source and audit.} This is the characteristic-two construction from
Shoup~\cite{Sho90}, using the squarefree factorization oracle and the two
prime-power branches before coprime assembly. Exact guards for the constructor
and its effective consumers exclude the former Shoup axiom and any replacement
nonstandard axiom. The companion ledger's historical admission is unchanged.

\begin{lemma}[Effective polynomial-basis field arithmetic]
\label{lem:effective-binary-field-arithmetic}
\ledgernode{1.1.6.1.2}
\lean{MIPRE.LowDegree.BinaryPolynomial.normalize_monic,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.BinaryPolynomial.evalBits_xor,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.BinaryPolynomial.evalBits_mulReduce,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.BinaryPolynomial.xorBitsProg,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.BinaryPolynomial.mulReduceProg,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.BinaryPolynomial.shoupLowerCoeffs_length,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.BinaryPolynomial.shoupLowerCoeffs_poly,
  MIPRE.SAT.quotientBinField,
  MIPRE.SAT.quotientBinField_toBits_ofBits,
  MIPRE.SAT.quotientBinField_ofBits,
  MIPRE.SAT.quotientBinField_add, MIPRE.SAT.quotientBinField_mul,
  MIPRE.SAT.shoupBinField, MIPRE.SAT.shoupAdmissibleField,
  MIPRE.SAT.shoupMulProg_time_le, MIPRE.SAT.shoupMulProg_correct}
\leanok
For a monic irreducible binary polynomial $f$ of degree $k$, its quotient field
has a canonical $k$-bit power-basis representation. Uniform ambient programs on
coefficient lists implement addition and multiplication in that field.
Using the proved Shoup construction, one uniform multiplication program
also constructs the modulus from unary $k$, and runs in time polynomial in $k$
on $k$-bit operands. This supplies a field family for every admissible odd $k$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
\leanok
XOR implements addition. Shift a coefficient vector and, when its highest bit
is one, XOR the lower coefficients of the monic modulus. Horner multiplication
uses this reduction after each shift. The accumulator length never increases,
so the ambient fold has a polynomial size and time bound. Horner evaluation
at a root of $f$ proves correctness, and the quotient's power basis proves
that correctly sized bit vectors are canonical. Normalize Shoup's output by
taking its first $k$ coefficients and treating the leading monic coefficient
implicitly. Compose the programs; the encoded input has size at most $10k+5$.
\end{proof}
\paragraph{Scope.} This is the addition and multiplication part of the
effective-field node. Lemma~\ref{lem:effective-field-inverse-trace} supplies element
inversion and trace computation. Matrix algorithms, the normal-element construction
and transport to a self-dual normal basis remain open.
The supplied-modulus arithmetic and the uniform field constructor use only Lean's standard axioms.

\begin{lemma}[Effective inversion, Frobenius iteration and trace]
\label{lem:effective-field-inverse-trace}
\ledgernode{1.1.6.1.2,1.1.6.1.3}
\uses{lem:effective-binary-field-arithmetic}
\lean{MIPRE.LowDegree.BinaryPolynomial.powerBitsProg,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.BinaryPolynomial.evalBits_powerBits,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.BinaryPolynomial.evalBits_inverseBits,
  MIPRE.SAT.shoupInvProg_correct,
  MIPRE.SAT.shoupInvProg_time_le,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.BinaryPolynomial.frobeniusTraceProg,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.BinaryPolynomial.evalBits_frobeniusTrace,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.BinaryPolynomial.evalBits_frobeniusTrace_trace,
  MIPRE.SAT.shoupBinField_finrank,
  MIPRE.SAT.shoupFrobeniusTraceProg_frobenius,
  MIPRE.SAT.shoupFrobeniusTraceProg_period,
  MIPRE.SAT.shoupTraceProg_correct,
  MIPRE.SAT.shoupTraceProg_time_le}
\leanok
Uniform ambient programs compute binary exponentiation, inversion of nonzero
field elements, iterated Frobenius, and the trace in the Shoup polynomial-basis
field. Inversion and trace computation, including construction of the modulus,
take polynomial time in the unary extension degree $k$ on $k$-bit operands.
After $k$ squarings an element returns to itself, and the sum of its first $k$
Frobenius iterates is its algebraic trace embedded in the extension field.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
\leanok
Binary exponentiation reads the exponent bit by bit, squaring the current power
and multiplying the accumulator when the bit is one. For a nonzero operand use
the exponent $2^k-2$, constructed directly as a $k$-bit string. Fixed-width
accumulators give a polynomial bound even on malformed inputs. A second loop
squares its current element and XORs it into an accumulator. Induction identifies
the accumulated sum, and the finite-field trace formula identifies it with the
algebraic trace. The concrete programs preserve the canonical quotient encoding.
\end{proof}
\paragraph{Scope.} These are element operations. Effective matrix construction,
Lemma~\ref{lem:effective-normal-element} constructs the normal element, and
Lemma~\ref{lem:effective-normal-selfdualization} supplies the effective self-dualizing
basis change. They are composed in Lemma~\ref{lem:self-dual-basis}. Lemma~\ref{lem:effective-field-matrices} supplies the Frobenius matrix,
inverse Gram computation and multiplication-table transport in any supplied basis. Lemma~\ref{lem:effective-binary-linear-algebra}
supplies the binary matrix operations, elimination and solves.

\label{lem:effective-binary-linear-algebra}

  MIPRE.LowDegree.BinaryLinear.kernel_generators_span,

  MIPRE.LowDegree.BinaryLinear.kernelGeneratorsProg_encoding,

\leanok
Uniform polynomial-time ambient programs compute binary vector addition and dot
products, matrix application, transposition and multiplication, a basis of the
span of a supplied family, solutions of consistent binary linear systems,
inverses of nonsingular binary matrices, and generators of matrix kernels. Explicit output dimensions are supplied
in unary. Basis rows retain coefficient certificates in the original family;
the returned vectors are independent, span exactly that family, and number at
most the ambient dimension. Inverse output uses canonical binary coordinates
and satisfies both multiplication identities.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
\leanok
Reduce each candidate against the stored pivots, XORing its coefficient certificate
at every step, and append a nonzero remainder at its first nonzero coordinate.
Later rows vanish at every earlier pivot. This triangular invariant proves
independence; reduction preserves membership in every subspace containing the
stored rows, which proves equality of spans. Reducing a target with an initially
zero certificate gives its coefficients in the original generating family.
Apply this solver to the columns of a matrix and to each standard basis vector
to construct an inverse. Each reduction preserves or shrinks both row widths,
and insertion adds a row of size bounded by the candidate's size. These bounds
prove polynomial runtime on all raw inputs, including malformed ones.
\end{proof}
Reducing the graph of the original matrix map and retaining the certificate
component gives a linear projection onto its kernel. Its images of the standard
basis are the computed kernel generators; they number exactly the input dimension
and may include zero or dependent vectors.
\paragraph{Scope.} The normal-element construction remains separate. These programs
supply the kernels, solves and matrix inverse needed by that construction.
The field-matrix applications are assembled in Lemma~\ref{lem:effective-field-matrices}.

\label{lem:effective-field-matrices}

The canonical Shoup-field coordinates form a binary linear equivalence. Uniform
polynomial-time ambient programs construct the binary Frobenius matrix, the trace
Gram matrix of a supplied family, its inverse when that family is a basis,
and coordinates and multiplication tables in any supplied basis. The Frobenius
matrix has minimal polynomial $X^k-1$, has $k$th power equal to the identity, and
has squarefree minimal polynomial when $k$ is odd. Constructing the Frobenius
matrix and printing all $k^3$ multiplication-table bits take polynomial time in $k$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
\leanok
Canonical encoding and decoding identify field vectors with binary coordinates.
Evaluate a linear map on the standard coordinate vectors to compute its matrix;
apply this to squaring. Transport Mathlib's Frobenius minimal-polynomial theorem
through the basis matrix equivalence. Extract the binary trace from its field
encoding, then evaluate it on each pairwise product. Nondegeneracy of the finite
field trace form makes the Gram matrix of a basis nonsingular. Binary elimination
computes its inverse and the coordinates of every basis-vector product. The
encoded size of a supplied basis is quadratic in $k$, so the ambient polynomial
bounds give the stated source bounds.
\end{proof}
\paragraph{Scope.} These programs consume a supplied basis. The self-dualizing
basis change is assembled in Lemma~\ref{lem:effective-normal-selfdualization};
Lemma~\ref{lem:effective-normal-element} constructs the initial normal element.

\begin{lemma}[Effective normal element by fixed-space projections]
\label{lem:effective-normal-element}
\ledgernode{1.1.6.1.6}
\lean{MIPRE.LowDegree.PrimitiveBinaryComponent.exists_mul_eq,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.splitAllComponents_primitive,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.ComponentFamily.length_le_finrank,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.BinaryLinear.groupFixedGeneratorsProg_correct,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.BinaryLinear.groupComponents_primitive,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.BinaryLinear.groupComponents_length_le,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.BinaryLinear.groupComponentsProg,
  MIPRE.LowDegree.BinaryLinear.groupComponentsProg_correct,
  MIPRE.SAT.shoupFrobeniusAction_injective,
  MIPRE.SAT.shoupProjectedVector_ne_zero,
  MIPRE.SAT.shoupNormalElement_projection,
  MIPRE.SAT.shoupNormalOrbitMap_injective,
  MIPRE.SAT.shoupNormalBasis_normal,
  MIPRE.SAT.shoupOrbitProg_correct,
  MIPRE.SAT.shoupActionProg_correct,
  MIPRE.SAT.shoupNormalElementProg_correct,
  MIPRE.SAT.shoupNormalBasisProg,
  MIPRE.SAT.shoupNormalBasisProg_correct}
\leanok
For odd $k>0$, a deterministic ambient program, with unary degree as its only
input, outputs the polynomial-basis encodings of a normal basis of the Shoup field.
Its runtime is polynomial in $k$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
\leanok
In $\F_2[C_k]$, compute generators of the fixed space of squaring by binary
elimination. Starting from $1$, split each component by a fixed generator $b$
into $eb$ and $e(1-b)$, omitting zeros. The remaining components are nonzero
orthogonal idempotents summing to $1$, so there are at most $k$. Spanning the
fixed space makes them primitive. Odd $k$ makes squaring bijective, and every
nonzero element supported on a primitive component has an inverse there.

Let the cyclic algebra act on the quotient field by Frobenius. Independence of
distinct field automorphisms makes this action faithful. For each component,
scan the polynomial basis and select its first nonzero projected vector. Their
sum has trivial annihilator: applying a component and its inverse would otherwise
kill its selected nonzero vector. Its Frobenius orbit is therefore a basis.
All scans, kernel computations, cyclic products and orbit computations are
implemented by polynomial-time programs. A dimension cap bounds component lists
also on malformed inputs and is proved inert on valid component families.
\end{proof}
\paragraph{Implementation.} This performs the paper's fixed-space separation
directly in the cyclic algebra. It constructs the CRT projections without
printing polynomial factors or calling polynomial gcd; no standalone binary
squarefree-factorization algorithm is claimed.

\begin{lemma}[Effective self-dualization of a supplied normal basis]
\label{lem:effective-normal-selfdualization}

  MIPRE.Cost.recordIteratesProg_apply,
